    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, I guess what brings me in is... I just can't get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. I mean, I know I've worked outdoors my whole life. You know, back in '88 we had this terrible drought, and I remember spending fifteen hours a day out on the tractor. The dust was just unbelievable that year, got right into the engine block of the old John Deere. I actually had to rebuild the carburetor myself, which I learned a bit about when I took a few semesters up at the state college before I came back to run the farm. 

Anyway... they don't make tractors like they used to. But yeah, my skin. The cracks, the spots, the sagging. It's all I see. Whenever I have to go into town, like if I'm talking to a banker or one of the county people, I just feel like they're staring right at my face and my hands. Just staring at how ruined they are. My wife says I'm making a big deal out of nothing and that I just look like a man who's worked outdoors. She's a good woman, my wife. We actually met at the county fair back in the day, she was showing these massive heirloom tomatoes and I was helping with the livestock... but anyway, yeah. I just can't stop focusing on how awful I look to everyone else, no matter what she says.

Well, yeah, it does get in the way. I mean, I've actually missed calls with the bankers, important ones, too. And I've put off big decisions about the farm just because I couldn't get myself looking acceptable enough to sit down with people. I just couldn't face them with my skin looking the way it does. 

Speaking of the farm, we've actually been trying to diversify lately. You know, with the crop yields being so unpredictable the last few years, we thought we'd try hosting some agritourism stuff. We set up this little retreat area down by the creek, and my wife's been selling her crafts there. She makes these beautiful woven baskets out of willow branches she gathers herself. It's really something to see. We had a guy from the local paper come out to do a story on it, and he wanted to take some pictures of us to help promote the retreat and the craft sales. But I just... I couldn't do it. I stopped agreeing to be in any of the photos. I just couldn't stand the thought of my face and hands being in the paper for everyone to see. So, she had to do the whole interview by herself. She was a little upset with me, but I just couldn't do it.

Distress? I mean, I don't know if I'd use those words exactly. I'm not sitting around crying or anything, if that's what you mean. It's just... well, like I said with the bankers and the photos, I just prefer to stay out of the way. As far as interacting goes, I still talk to people when I absolutely have to, like down at the feed store. 

Though, honestly, going down there isn't what it used to be anyway. Old man Miller finally retired last spring and sold the place to some big corporate outfit out of Omaha. Now you go in, and they don't even stock the right sweet feed for the calves anymore. I was trying to explain it to the new manager last week—young kid, maybe twenty-five, wearing these brand-new leather boots that have clearly never seen a day of mud in their life—and I asked him if they could special order the old mix we've used since the nineties. He just pulls out this little tablet screen, taps on it a few times, and tells me it's not in their system. Just like that! Didn't even offer to call the distributor. It's a real shame how all the local places are getting bought up by people who don't know the first thing about livestock. You used to be able to just shake a man's hand and get things done.

But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, interacting with folks. Yeah, I don't know about a lot of emotional distress, I just really hate the thought of people staring at how ruined and cracked my skin is when I'm just trying to do business with them.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess what brings me in is... I just can't get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. I mean, I know I've worked outdoors my whole life. You know, back in '88 we had this terrible drought, and I remember spending fifteen hours a day out on the tractor. The dust was just unbelievable that year, got right into the engine block of the old John Deere. I actually had to rebuild the carburetor myself, which I learned a bit about when I took a few semesters up at the state college before I came back to run the farm. \n\nAnyway... they don't make tractors like they used to. But yeah, my skin. The cracks, the spots, the sagging. It's all I see. Whenever I have to go into town, like if I'm talking to a banker or one of the county people, I just feel like they're staring right at my face and my hands. Just staring at how ruined they are. My wife says I'm making a big deal out of nothing and that I just look like a man who's worked outdoors. She's a good woman, my wife. We actually met at the county fair back in the day, she was showing these massive heirloom tomatoes and I was helping with the livestock... but anyway, yeah. I just can't stop focusing on how awful I look to everyone else, no matter what she says.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your skin and appearance ever get so intense that you have trouble focusing on anything else, or do they interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it does get in the way. I mean, I've actually missed calls with the bankers, important ones, too. And I've put off big decisions about the farm just because I couldn't get myself looking acceptable enough to sit down with people. I just couldn't face them with my skin looking the way it does. \n\nSpeaking of the farm, we've actually been trying to diversify lately. You know, with the crop yields being so unpredictable the last few years, we thought we'd try hosting some agritourism stuff. We set up this little retreat area down by the creek, and my wife's been selling her crafts there. She makes these beautiful woven baskets out of willow branches she gathers herself. It's really something to see. We had a guy from the local paper come out to do a story on it, and he wanted to take some pictures of us to help promote the retreat and the craft sales. But I just... I couldn't do it. I stopped agreeing to be in any of the photos. I just couldn't stand the thought of my face and hands being in the paper for everyone to see. So, she had to do the whole interview by herself. She was a little upset with me, but I just couldn't do it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your appearance ever make it hard for you to interact with others, or cause you a lot of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Distress? I mean, I don't know if I'd use those words exactly. I'm not sitting around crying or anything, if that's what you mean. It's just... well, like I said with the bankers and the photos, I just prefer to stay out of the way. As far as interacting goes, I still talk to people when I absolutely have to, like down at the feed store. \n\nThough, honestly, going down there isn't what it used to be anyway. Old man Miller finally retired last spring and sold the place to some big corporate outfit out of Omaha. Now you go in, and they don't even stock the right sweet feed for the calves anymore. I was trying to explain it to the new manager last week—young kid, maybe twenty-five, wearing these brand-new leather boots that have clearly never seen a day of mud in their life—and I asked him if they could special order the old mix we've used since the nineties. He just pulls out this little tablet screen, taps on it a few times, and tells me it's not in their system. Just like that! Didn't even offer to call the distributor. It's a real shame how all the local places are getting bought up by people who don't know the first thing about livestock. You used to be able to just shake a man's hand and get things done.\n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, interacting with folks. Yeah, I don't know about a lot of emotional distress, I just really hate the thought of people staring at how ruined and cracked my skin is when I'm just trying to do business with them.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever avoid social situations or feel very anxious in settings where people might see your face or hands?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, avoid social situations... I guess you could say that. I mean, I don't go out of my way to be around crowds if I don't have to. Like the church picnic last month. I just couldn't stomach the idea of sitting there with a plate of barbecue while everyone's looking at my hands holding the fork, seeing every little crack and sunspot. 

You know, speaking of barbecue, Pastor Dave always insists on smoking the briskets himself, but he uses this green hickory wood that just gives it this bitter, acrid taste. I tried telling him a few years back that he needs to let the wood season properly—at least six to eight months, really—before he puts it in the smoker. My granddad had a smokehouse out back of the old property, and he'd cure these hams that would just melt in your mouth. He always said the secret was a mix of applewood and just a little bit of cherry. We used to have this huge cherry tree near the front porch... lightning struck it back in '96, split it right down the middle. Broke my heart to chop it up for firewood, but we didn't have much choice. 

But yeah, anxious? I don't know if anxious is the right word. I just don't want people looking at me. So if I don't have to be there, I just stay home.

Old man Miller finally retired last spring and sold the place to some big corporate outfit out of Omaha. Now you go in, and they don't even stock the right sweet feed for the calves anymore. I was trying to explain it to the new manager last week—young kid, maybe twenty-five, wearing these brand-new leather boots that have clearly never seen a day of mud in their life—and I asked him if they could special order the old mix we've used since the nineties. He just pulls out this little tablet screen, taps on it a few times, and tells me it's not in their system. Just like that! Didn't even offer to call the distributor. It's a real shame how all the local places are getting bought up by people who don't know the first thing about livestock. I guess you could say that. I mean, I don't go out of my way to be around crowds if I don't have to. Like the church picnic last month. I just couldn't stomach the idea of sitting there with a plate of barbecue while everyone's looking at my hands holding the fork, seeing every little crack and sunspot. \n\nYou know, speaking of barbecue, Pastor Dave always insists on smoking the briskets himself, but he uses this green hickory wood that just gives it this bitter, acrid taste. I tried telling him a few years back that he needs to let the wood season properly—at least six to eight months, really—before he puts it in the smoker. My granddad had a smokehouse out back of the old property, and he'd cure these hams that would just melt in your mouth. He always said the secret was a mix of applewood and just a little bit of cherry. We used to have this huge cherry tree near the front porch... lightning struck it back in '96, split it right down the middle. Broke my heart to chop it up for firewood, but we didn't have much choice. \n\nBut yeah, anxious? I don't know if anxious is the right word. I just don't want people looking at me. So if I don't have to be there, I just stay home.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're in situations where people might see your face or hands, do you feel very nervous or anxious about how they might react?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, nervous... I guess a little, sure. Mostly I just feel... exposed, you know? Like they're just staring right at the damage. It's why I started wearing those light cotton gloves when I go into town, even in the summer. 

Though, finding good cotton gloves anymore is a chore in itself. Used to be you could go down to the hardware store and buy a pack of ten for next to nothing. Good, sturdy canvas ones, too. Now everything's that synthetic nylon stuff that makes your hands sweat out in the fields. I bought a pair last month, and within two days of fixing fence posts, the seams were already blowing out. It's like nobody knows how to stitch a proper seam anymore. My grandmother used to make all our work shirts on this heavy iron Singer machine she had by the window. She could sew through denim like it was butter. I still have one of those shirts somewhere in the attic... probably smells like mothballs and dust now. 

But, uh, what were you asking? Oh, about how people react. Yeah, I don't know if I'm nervous exactly, I just... I just know they're looking, and I hate it. So I just try to keep my head down and get my business done quick.

I mean, I don't know if I'd use those words exactly. I'm not sitting around crying or anything, if that's what you mean. It's just... well, like I said with the bankers and the photos, I just prefer to stay out of the way. As far as interacting goes, I still talk to people when I absolutely have to, like down at the feed store. \n\nThough, honestly, going down there isn't what it used to be anyway. Old man Miller finally retired last spring and sold the place to some big corporate outfit out of Omaha. Now you go in, and they don't even stock the right sweet feed for the calves anymore. I was trying to explain it to the new manager last week—young kid, maybe twenty-five, wearing these brand-new leather boots that have clearly never seen a day of mud in their life—and I asked him if they could special order the old mix we've used since the nineties. He just pulls out this little tablet screen, taps on it a few times, and tells me it's not in their system. Just like that! Didn't even offer to call the distributor. Yeah, I don't know about a lot of emotional distress, I just really hate the thought of people staring at how ruined and cracked my skin is when I'm just trying to do business with them.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid social situations or feel very anxious in settings where people might see your face or hands?\n\nPatient: Well, avoid social situations... I guess you could say that. I mean, I don't go out of my way to be around crowds if I don't have to. Like the church picnic last month. I just couldn't stomach the idea of sitting there with a plate of barbecue while everyone's looking at my hands holding the fork, seeing every little crack and sunspot. \n\nYou know, speaking of barbecue, Pastor Dave always insists on smoking the briskets himself, but he uses this green hickory wood that just gives it this bitter, acrid taste. I tried telling him a few years back that he needs to let the wood season properly—at least six to eight months, really—before he puts it in the smoker. My granddad had a smokehouse out back of the old property, and he'd cure these hams that would just melt in your mouth. He always said the secret was a mix of applewood and just a little bit of cherry. We used to have this huge cherry tree near the front porch... lightning struck it back in '96, split it right down the middle. Broke my heart to chop it up for firewood, but we didn't have much choice. \n\nBut yeah, anxious? I don't know if anxious is the right word. I just don't want people looking at me. So if I don't have to be there, I just stay home.\n\nDoctor: When you're in situations where people might see your face or hands, do you feel very nervous or anxious about how they might react?\n\nPatient: Well, nervous... It's like nobody knows how to stitch a proper seam anymore. My grandmother used to make all our work shirts on this heavy iron Singer machine she had by the window. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Judged? I don't... I mean, I'm not really worried about them judging me, like they think I'm a bad person or something. It's more just... uncomfortable, like you said. I know how bad the skin looks, all leathery and ruined, and I just don't want them having to look at it. 

You know, speaking of leather, I used to do a bit of tooling myself, back in the day. Made my own belts, even did a saddle once. Takes a lot of patience, getting the moisture just right before you start stamping the pattern. You let it get too dry, and it cracks—just like my hands, I suppose. I had this beautiful set of stamping tools I bought off a fella in Cheyenne... must have been 1982? Yeah, '82, because that was the year the truck broke down on the way back. Blew a head gasket right outside of Laramie. Had to sit at this little diner for six hours waiting for a tow. Best chicken fried steak I ever had, though. The gravy was real thick, not that watery stuff you get out of a packet nowadays. 

But no, I wouldn't say I'm scared of being embarrassed or judged. I just... I know it's ugly, and I just prefer to keep it to myself.

I was trying to explain it to the new manager last week—young kid, maybe twenty-five, wearing these brand-new leather boots that have clearly never seen a day of mud in their life—and I asked him if they could special order the old mix we've used since the nineties. He just pulls out this little tablet screen, taps on it a few times, and tells me it's not in their system. Just like that! Didn't even offer to call the distributor. It's a real shame how all the local places are getting bought up by people who don't know the first thing about livestock. You used to be able to just shake a man's hand and get things done.\n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, interacting with folks. Yeah, I don't know about a lot of emotional distress, I just really hate the thought of people staring at how ruined and cracked my skin is when I'm just trying to do business with them.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid social situations or feel very anxious in settings where people might see your face or hands?\n\nPatient: Well, avoid social situations... I guess you could say that. I mean, I don't go out of my way to be around crowds if I don't have to. Like the church picnic last month. I just couldn't stomach the idea of sitting there with a plate of barbecue while everyone's looking at my hands holding the fork, seeing every little crack and sunspot. \n\nYou know, speaking of barbecue, Pastor Dave always insists on smoking the briskets himself, but he uses this green hickory wood that just gives it this bitter, acrid taste. I tried telling him a few years back that he needs to let the wood season properly—at least six to eight months, really—before he puts it in the smoker. My granddad had a smokehouse out back of the old property, and he'd cure these hams that would just melt in your mouth. He always said the secret was a mix of applewood and just a little bit of cherry. We used to have this huge cherry tree near the front porch... lightning struck it back in '96, split it right down the middle. Broke my heart to chop it up for firewood, but we didn't have much choice. \n\nBut yeah, anxious? I don't know if anxious is the right word. I just don't want people looking at me. So if I don't have to be there, I just stay home.\n\nDoctor: When you're in situations where people might see your face or hands, do you feel very nervous or anxious about how they might react?\n\nPatient: Well, nervous... I guess a little, sure. Mostly I just feel... exposed, you know? Like they're just staring right at the damage. It's why I started wearing those light cotton gloves when I go into town, even in the summer. \n\nThough, finding good cotton gloves anymore is a chore in itself. Used to be you could go down to the hardware store and buy a pack of ten for next to nothing. Good, sturdy canvas ones, too. Now everything's that synthetic nylon stuff that makes your hands sweat out in the fields. I bought a pair last month, and within two days of fixing fence posts, the seams were already blowing out. It's like nobody knows how to stitch a proper seam anymore. My grandmother used to make all our work shirts on this heavy iron Singer machine she had by the window. She could sew through denim like it was butter. I still have one of those shirts somewhere in the attic... probably smells like mothballs and dust now. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, about how people react. Yeah, I don't know if I'm nervous exactly, I just... I just know they're looking, and I hate it. So I just try to keep my head down and get my business done quick.\n\nDoctor: When you're in public and people might see your face or hands, do you feel a strong fear of being judged or embarrassed, or do you mostly just feel uncomfortable with how you look?\n\nPatient: Judged? I don't... I mean, I'm not really worried about them judging me, like they think I'm a bad person or something. It's more just... uncomfortable, like you said. I know how bad the skin looks, all leathery and ruined, and I just don't want them having to look at it. \n\nYou know, speaking of leather, I used to do a bit of tooling myself, back in the day. Made my own belts, even did a saddle once. Takes a lot of patience, getting the moisture just right before you start stamping the pattern. You let it get too dry, and it cracks—just like my hands, I suppose. I had this beautiful set of stamping tools I bought off a fella in Cheyenne... must have been 1982? Yeah, '82, because that was the year the truck broke down on the way back. Blew a head gasket right outside of Laramie. Had to sit at this little diner for six hours waiting for a tow. Best chicken fried steak I ever had, though. The gravy was real thick, not that watery stuff you get out of a packet nowadays. \n\nBut no, I wouldn't say I'm scared of being embarrassed or judged. I just... I know it's ugly, and I just prefer to keep it to myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Panic? No, I wouldn't call it panic. I mean, if I'm at the feed store and I realize I left my gloves in the truck, I definitely want to just grab my bags and get out of there as fast as I can. It's just... a really strong urge to just not be seen, you know? 

Like last Tuesday, I was picking up some sweet feed for the horses. The guy loading the bags—young kid, maybe nineteen, wearing one of those baseball caps with the flat bill—he kept looking down at my hands when I handed him the receipt. Kids these days, they don't have the same work ethic, you know? Back when I was his age, working summers on the old Miller farm, we were up at four in the morning pitching hay. Old man Miller, he was a tough son of a gun. Lost two fingers in a combine accident back in the fifties, but he could still outwork any of us. He used to chew this awful black licorice tobacco. Swore it kept the dust out of his throat. I tried it once, nearly made me sick to my stomach. Tasted like... well, tasted like burnt rubber and molasses. 

But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, leaving. Yeah, if I can't cover up, I just want to finish up and go home. I don't start hyperventilating or anything, I just... I just don't want to linger.

Watched or evaluated? No, I don't think so. I mean, I know they're looking at the damage, but I don't feel like they're evaluating me, like giving me a grade or something. It's not like when the county inspector comes out to look at the new barn. 

Boy, that was a headache and a half last spring. The inspector they sent out, this guy named Gary, he had to measure every single joist. Took him three hours. And he had this clipboard that he kept tapping his pen against, click-clack, click-clack. Drove my dog Buster absolutely crazy. Buster's a blue heeler mix, smart as a whip, but he hates repetitive noises. He'll bark at the washing machine if it gets off balance. We had a Maytag for twenty years that finally gave up the ghost, and the new one we got has all these digital buttons that beep every time you touch them. I don't know why a washing machine needs to sound like a video game. 

But anyway, no, I don't feel like people are judging me like that. I just... I know my skin looks terrible, and I just prefer to keep it hidden so nobody has to stare at it.

I just couldn't face them with my skin looking the way it does. \n\nSpeaking of the farm, we've actually been trying to diversify lately. You know, with the crop yields being so unpredictable the last few years, we thought we'd try hosting some agritourism stuff. We set up this little retreat area down by the creek, and my wife's been selling her crafts there. She makes these beautiful woven baskets out of willow branches she gathers herself. It's really something to see. We had a guy from the local paper come out to do a story on it, and he wanted to take some pictures of us to help promote the retreat and the craft sales. But I just... I couldn't do it. I stopped agreeing to be in any of the photos. I just couldn't stand the thought of my face and hands being in the paper for everyone to see. So, she had to do the whole interview by herself. She was a little upset with me, but I just couldn't do it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your appearance ever make it hard for you to interact with others, or cause you a lot of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Distress? I mean, I don't know if I'd use those words exactly. I'm not sitting around crying or anything, if that's what you mean. It's just... well, like I said with the bankers and the photos, I just prefer to stay out of the way. As far as interacting goes, I still talk to people when I absolutely have to, like down at the feed store. \n\nThough, honestly, going down there isn't what it used to be anyway. Old man Miller finally retired last spring and sold the place to some big corporate outfit out of Omaha. Now you go in, and they don't even stock the right sweet feed for the calves anymore. I was trying to explain it to the new manager last week—young kid, maybe twenty-five, wearing these brand-new leather boots that have clearly never seen a day of mud in their life—and I asked him if they could special order the old mix we've used since the nineties. He just pulls out this little tablet screen, taps on it a few times, and tells me it's not in their system. Just like that! Didn't even offer to call the distributor. It's a real shame how all the local places are getting bought up by people who don't know the first thing about livestock. You used to be able to just shake a man's hand and get things done.\n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, interacting with folks. Yeah, I don't know about a lot of emotional distress, I just really hate the thought of people staring at how ruined and cracked my skin is when I'm just trying to do business with them.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid social situations or feel very anxious in settings where people might see your face or hands?\n\nPatient: Well, avoid social situations... I guess you could say that. I mean, I don't go out of my way to be around crowds if I don't have to. Like the church picnic last month. I just couldn't stomach the idea of sitting there with a plate of barbecue while everyone's looking at my hands holding the fork, seeing every little crack and sunspot. \n\nYou know, speaking of barbecue, Pastor Dave always insists on smoking the briskets himself, but he uses this green hickory wood that just gives it this bitter, acrid taste. I tried telling him a few years back that he needs to let the wood season properly—at least six to eight months, really—before he puts it in the smoker. My granddad had a smokehouse out back of the old property, and he'd cure these hams that would just melt in your mouth. He always said the secret was a mix of applewood and just a little bit of cherry. We used to have this huge cherry tree near the front porch... lightning struck it back in '96, split it right down the middle. Broke my heart to chop it up for firewood, but we didn't have much choice. \n\nBut yeah, anxious? I don't know if anxious is the right word. I just don't want people looking at me. So if I don't have to be there, I just stay home.\n\nDoctor: When you're in situations where people might see your face or hands, do you feel very nervous or anxious about how they might react?\n\nPatient: Well, nervous... I guess a little, sure. Mostly I just feel... exposed, you know? Like they're just staring right at the damage. It's why I started wearing those light cotton gloves when I go into town, even in the summer. \n\nThough, finding good cotton gloves anymore is a chore in itself. Used to be you could go down to the hardware store and buy a pack of ten for next to nothing. Good, sturdy canvas ones, too. Now everything's that synthetic nylon stuff that makes your hands sweat out in the fields. I bought a pair last month, and within two days of fixing fence posts, the seams were already blowing out. It's like nobody knows how to stitch a proper seam anymore. My grandmother used to make all our work shirts on this heavy iron Singer machine she had by the window. She could sew through denim like it was butter. I still have one of those shirts somewhere in the attic... probably smells like mothballs and dust now. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, about how people react. Yeah, I don't know if I'm nervous exactly, I just... I just know they're looking, and I hate it. So I just try to keep my head down and get my business done quick.\n\nDoctor: When you're in public and people might see your face or hands, do you feel a strong fear of being judged or embarrassed, or do you mostly just feel uncomfortable with how you look?\n\nPatient: Judged? I don't... I mean, I'm not really worried about them judging me, like they think I'm a bad person or something. It's more just... uncomfortable, like you said. I know how bad the skin looks, all leathery and ruined, and I just don't want them having to look at it. \n\nYou know, speaking of leather, I used to do a bit of tooling myself, back in the day. Made my own belts, even did a saddle once. Takes a lot of patience, getting the moisture just right before you start stamping the pattern. You let it get too dry, and it cracks—just like my hands, I suppose. I had this beautiful set of stamping tools I bought off a fella in Cheyenne... must have been 1982? Yeah, '82, because that was the year the truck broke down on the way back. Blew a head gasket right outside of Laramie. Had to sit at this little diner for six hours waiting for a tow. Best chicken fried steak I ever had, though. The gravy was real thick, not that watery stuff you get out of a packet nowadays. \n\nBut no, I wouldn't say I'm scared of being embarrassed or judged. I just... I know it's ugly, and I just prefer to keep it to myself.\n\nDoctor: When you're in public and can't cover your hands or face, do you feel a strong urge to leave or a sense of panic about how people might react?\n\nPatient: Panic? No, I wouldn't call it panic. I mean, if I'm at the feed store and I realize I left my gloves in the truck, I definitely want to just grab my bags and get out of there as fast as I can. It's just... a really strong urge to just not be seen, you know? \n\nLike last Tuesday, I was picking up some sweet feed for the horses. The guy loading the bags—young kid, maybe nineteen, wearing one of those baseball caps with the flat bill—he kept looking down at my hands when I handed him the receipt. Kids these days, they don't have the same work ethic, you know? Back when I was his age, working summers on the old Miller farm, we were up at four in the morning pitching hay. Old man Miller, he was a tough son of a gun. Lost two fingers in a combine accident back in the fifties, but he could still outwork any of us. He used to chew this awful black licorice tobacco. Swore it kept the dust out of his throat. I tried it once, nearly made me sick to my stomach. Tasted like... well, tasted like burnt rubber and molasses. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, leaving. Yeah, if I can't cover up, I just want to finish up and go home. I don't start hyperventilating or anything, I just... I just don't want to linger.\n\nDoctor: When you're in public and people might see your face or hands, do you ever feel a strong fear of being watched, judged, or evaluated by others because of how you look?\n\nPatient: Watched or evaluated? No, I don't think so. I mean, I know they're looking at the damage, but I don't feel like they're evaluating me, like giving me a grade or something. It's not like when the county inspector comes out to look at the new barn. \n\nBoy, that was a headache and a half last spring. The inspector they sent out, this guy named Gary, he had to measure every single joist. Took him three hours. And he had this clipboard that he kept tapping his pen against, click-clack, click-clack. Drove my dog Buster absolutely crazy. Buster's a blue heeler mix, smart as a whip, but he hates repetitive noises. He'll bark at the washing machine if it gets off balance. We had a Maytag for twenty years that finally gave up the ghost, and the new one we got has all these digital buttons that beep every time you touch them. I don't know why a washing machine needs to sound like a video game. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't feel like people are judging me like that. I just... I know my skin looks terrible, and I just prefer to keep it hidden so nobody has to stare at it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Made my own belts, even did a saddle once. Takes a lot of patience, getting the moisture just right before you start stamping the pattern. You let it get too dry, and it cracks—just like my hands, I suppose. I had this beautiful set of stamping tools I bought off a fella in Cheyenne... must have been 1982? Yeah, '82, because that was the year the truck broke down on the way back. Blew a head gasket right outside of Laramie. Had to sit at this little diner for six hours waiting for a tow. Best chicken fried steak I ever had, though. The gravy was real thick, not that watery stuff you get out of a packet nowadays. \n\nBut no, I wouldn't say I'm scared of being embarrassed or judged. I just... I know it's ugly, and I just prefer to keep it to myself.\n\nDoctor: When you're in public and can't cover your hands or face, do you feel a strong urge to leave or a sense of panic about how people might react?\n\nPatient: Panic? No, I wouldn't call it panic. I mean, if I'm at the feed store and I realize I left my gloves in the truck, I definitely want to just grab my bags and get out of there as fast as I can. It's just... a really strong urge to just not be seen, you know? \n\nLike last Tuesday, I was picking up some sweet feed for the horses. The guy loading the bags—young kid, maybe nineteen, wearing one of those baseball caps with the flat bill—he kept looking down at my hands when I handed him the receipt. Kids these days, they don't have the same work ethic, you know? Back when I was his age, working summers on the old Miller farm, we were up at four in the morning pitching hay. Old man Miller, he was a tough son of a gun. Lost two fingers in a combine accident back in the fifties, but he could still outwork any of us. He used to chew this awful black licorice tobacco. Swore it kept the dust out of his throat. I tried it once, nearly made me sick to my stomach. Tasted like... well, tasted like burnt rubber and molasses. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, leaving. Yeah, if I can't cover up, I just want to finish up and go home. I don't start hyperventilating or anything, I just... I just don't want to linger.\n\nDoctor: When you're in public and people might see your face or hands, do you ever feel a strong fear of being watched, judged, or evaluated by others because of how you look?\n\nPatient: Watched or evaluated? No, I don't think so. I mean, I know they're looking at the damage, but I don't feel like they're evaluating me, like giving me a grade or something. It's not like when the county inspector comes out to look at the new barn. \n\nBoy, that was a headache and a half last spring. The inspector they sent out, this guy named Gary, he had to measure every single joist. Took him three hours. And he had this clipboard that he kept tapping his pen against, click-clack, click-clack. Drove my dog Buster absolutely crazy. Buster's a blue heeler mix, smart as a whip, but he hates repetitive noises. He'll bark at the washing machine if it gets off balance. We had a Maytag for twenty years that finally gave up the ghost, and the new one we got has all these digital buttons that beep every time you touch them. I don't know why a washing machine needs to sound like a video game. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't feel like people are judging me like that. I just... I know my skin looks terrible, and I just prefer to keep it hidden so nobody has to stare at it.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're in public and people might see your face or hands, do you ever feel a strong sense of anxiety or fear about how you think they might react to your appearance?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 62 / miscellaneous_agricultural_worker / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Anxiety or fear about their reaction? I don't know if I'd say anxiety. I mean, I know what they're gonna think because I know what it looks like, right? It's not a mystery. But I wouldn't say I'm sitting there shaking in my boots about it. 

You know, speaking of shaking, we had this old tractor when I was first starting out—an old John Deere, green as a spring apple. The seat on that thing would rattle so hard when you put it in third gear, you'd feel it in your teeth for an hour after you shut it off. My buddy Tom, he tried to fix it by wedging a piece of tire rubber under the mounting bracket. Tom was always doing stuff like that, rigging things up with baling wire and duct tape. He actually built a whole chicken coop using nothing but scrap wood and pallets he found behind the hardware store. Painted the whole thing barn red, but he ran out of paint halfway through the back wall, so it was just bare wood on one side. The chickens didn't seem to mind, though. They laid the biggest brown eggs you ever saw. 

But yeah, back to what you asked... I just don't like them seeing it. It's not a panic attack or anything, I just... I know it's ruined, and I'd rather just keep it covered up.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess what brings me in is... I just can't get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. I mean, I know I've worked outdoors my whole life. You know, back in '88 we had this terrible drought, and I remember spending fifteen hours a day out on the tractor. The dust was just unbelievable that year, got right into the engine block of the old John Deere. I actually had to rebuild the carburetor myself, which I learned a bit about when I took a few semesters up at the state college before I came back to run the farm. \n\nAnyway... they don't make tractors like they used to. But yeah, my skin. The cracks, the spots, the sagging. It's all I see. Whenever I have to go into town, like if I'm talking to a banker or one of the county people, I just feel like they're staring right at my face and my hands. Just staring at how ruined they are. My wife says I'm making a big deal out of nothing and that I just look like a man who's worked outdoors. She's a good woman, my wife. We actually met at the county fair back in the day, she was showing these massive heirloom tomatoes and I was helping with the livestock... but anyway, yeah. I just can't stop focusing on how awful I look to everyone else, no matter what she says.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your skin and appearance ever get so intense that you have trouble focusing on anything else, or do they interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it does get in the way. It's really something to see. We had a guy from the local paper come out to do a story on it, and he wanted to take some pictures of us to help promote the retreat and the craft sales. But I just... I couldn't do it. I stopped agreeing to be in any of the photos. I just couldn't stand the thought of my face and hands being in the paper for everyone to see. So, she had to do the whole interview by herself. She was a little upset with me, but I just couldn't do it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your appearance ever make it hard for you to interact with others, or cause you a lot of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Distress? I mean, I don't know if I'd use those words exactly. I'm not sitting around crying or anything, if that's what you mean. It's just... well, like I said with the bankers and the photos, I just prefer to stay out of the way. Old man Miller finally retired last spring and sold the place to some big corporate outfit out of Omaha. Now you go in, and they don't even stock the right sweet feed for the calves anymore. He just pulls out this little tablet screen, taps on it a few times, and tells me it's not in their system. Just like that! Didn't even offer to call the distributor. It's a real shame how all the local places are getting bought up by people who don't know the first thing about livestock. Yeah, I don't know about a lot of emotional distress, I just really hate the thought of people staring at how ruined and cracked my skin is when I'm just trying to do business with them.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid social situations or feel very anxious in settings where people might see your face or hands?\n\nPatient: Well, avoid social situations... I guess you could say that. I mean, I don't go out of my way to be around crowds if I don't have to. Like the church picnic last month. I just couldn't stomach the idea of sitting there with a plate of barbecue while everyone's looking at my hands holding the fork, seeing every little crack and sunspot. \n\nYou know, speaking of barbecue, Pastor Dave always insists on smoking the briskets himself, but he uses this green hickory wood that just gives it this bitter, acrid taste. I tried telling him a few years back that he needs to let the wood season properly—at least six to eight months, really—before he puts it in the smoker. My granddad had a smokehouse out back of the old property, and he'd cure these hams that would just melt in your mouth. He always said the secret was a mix of applewood and just a little bit of cherry. We used to have this huge cherry tree near the front porch... lightning struck it back in '96, split it right down the middle. Broke my heart to chop it up for firewood, but we didn't have much choice. \n\nBut yeah, anxious? I don't know if anxious is the right word. I just don't want people looking at me. So if I don't have to be there, I just stay home.\n\nDoctor: When you're in situations where people might see your face or hands, do you feel very nervous or anxious about how they might react?\n\nPatient: Well, nervous... I guess a little, sure. Mostly I just feel... exposed, you know? Like they're just staring right at the damage. It's why I started wearing those light cotton gloves when I go into town, even in the summer. \n\nThough, finding good cotton gloves anymore is a chore in itself. Used to be you could go down to the hardware store and buy a pack of ten for next to nothing. Good, sturdy canvas ones, too. Now everything's that synthetic nylon stuff that makes your hands sweat out in the fields. I bought a pair last month, and within two days of fixing fence posts, the seams were already blowing out. It's like nobody knows how to stitch a proper seam anymore. My grandmother used to make all our work shirts on this heavy iron Singer machine she had by the window. She could sew through denim like it was butter. I still have one of those shirts somewhere in the attic... probably smells like mothballs and dust now. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, about how people react. Yeah, I don't know if I'm nervous exactly, I just... I just know they're looking, and I hate it. Kids these days, they don't have the same work ethic, you know? Back when I was his age, working summers on the old Miller farm, we were up at four in the morning pitching hay. Old man Miller, he was a tough son of a gun. Lost two fingers in a combine accident back in the fifties, but he could still outwork any of us. He used to chew this awful black licorice tobacco. Swore it kept the dust out of his throat. I tried it once, nearly made me sick to my stomach. Tasted like... well, tasted like burnt rubber and molasses. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, leaving. Yeah, if I can't cover up, I just want to finish up and go home. I don't start hyperventilating or anything, I just... I just don't want to linger.\n\nDoctor: When you're in public and people might see your face or hands, do you ever feel a strong fear of being watched, judged, or evaluated by others because of how you look?\n\nPatient: Watched or evaluated? No, I don't think so. I mean, I know they're looking at the damage, but I don't feel like they're evaluating me, like giving me a grade or something. It's not like when the county inspector comes out to look at the new barn. \n\nBoy, that was a headache and a half last spring. The inspector they sent out, this guy named Gary, he had to measure every single joist. Took him three hours. And he had this clipboard that he kept tapping his pen against, click-clack, click-clack. Drove my dog Buster absolutely crazy. Buster's a blue heeler mix, smart as a whip, but he hates repetitive noises. He'll bark at the washing machine if it gets off balance. We had a Maytag for twenty years that finally gave up the ghost, and the new one we got has all these digital buttons that beep every time you touch them. I don't know why a washing machine needs to sound like a video game. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't feel like people are judging me like that. I just... I know my skin looks terrible, and I just prefer to keep it hidden so nobody has to stare at it.\n\nDoctor: When you're in public and people might see your face or hands, do you ever feel a strong sense of anxiety or fear about how you think they might react to your appearance?\n\nPatient: Anxiety or fear about their reaction? Didn't we just cover this one, Doc? Like I was saying just a second ago, it's not really fear or anxiety. I just know it looks terrible, so I keep it hidden. It's just practical, you know? Like... well, like throwing a tarp over the hay before a storm. You're not sitting in the house terrified of the rain, you just know what a mess it's gonna make if you don't cover up. 

Speaking of storms, we had a real nasty one roll through last July. Sky turned this weird bruised purple color right around supper time. My wife was making her meatloaf—she puts a little bit of brown sugar in the ketchup glaze, sounds crazy but it's fantastic—anyway, the wind picked up so fast it took the aluminum roof right off the old pump house. Just peeled it back like a sardine can. We found pieces of it three pastures over the next morning. And the hail! Looked like golf balls bouncing off the porch. Dented the hood of my Chevy so bad it looked like someone took a ball-peen hammer to the whole front end. The insurance adjuster they sent out, this fella with a terrible comb-over, tried to tell me it was pre-existing wear and tear. On a truck I bought brand new! I had to get the dealership on the phone just to sort the guy out. 

But anyway, yeah, no anxiety or fear like you're talking about. I just... I know the damage is there, and I just prefer to keep my hands in my pockets or wear my gloves so nobody has to look at it.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess what brings me in is... I just can't get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. I mean, I know I've worked outdoors my whole life. You know, back in '88 we had this terrible drought, and I remember spending fifteen hours a day out on the tractor. The dust was just unbelievable that year, got right into the engine block of the old John Deere. I actually had to rebuild the carburetor myself, which I learned a bit about when I took a few semesters up at the state college before I came back to run the farm. \n\nAnyway... they don't make tractors like they used to. But yeah, my skin. The cracks, the spots, the sagging. It's all I see. Whenever I have to go into town, like if I'm talking to a banker or one of the county people, I just feel like they're staring right at my face and my hands. Just staring at how ruined they are. My wife says I'm making a big deal out of nothing and that I just look like a man who's worked outdoors. She's a good woman, my wife. We actually met at the county fair back in the day, she was showing these massive heirloom tomatoes and I was helping with the livestock... but anyway, yeah. I just can't stop focusing on how awful I look to everyone else, no matter what she says.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your skin and appearance ever get so intense that you have trouble focusing on anything else, or do they interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it does get in the way. I mean, I've actually missed calls with the bankers, important ones, too. And I've put off big decisions about the farm just because I couldn't get myself looking acceptable enough to sit down with people. I just couldn't face them with my skin looking the way it does. \n\nSpeaking of the farm, we've actually been trying to diversify lately. You know, with the crop yields being so unpredictable the last few years, we thought we'd try hosting some agritourism stuff. We set up this little retreat area down by the creek, and my wife's been selling her crafts there. She makes these beautiful woven baskets out of willow branches she gathers herself. It's really something to see. We had a guy from the local paper come out to do a story on it, and he wanted to take some pictures of us to help promote the retreat and the craft sales. But I just... I couldn't do it. I stopped agreeing to be in any of the photos. I just couldn't stand the thought of my face and hands being in the paper for everyone to see. So, she had to do the whole interview by herself. She was a little upset with me, but I just couldn't do it.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your appearance ever make it hard for you to interact with others, or cause you a lot of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Distress? I mean, I don't know if I'd use those words exactly. I'm not sitting around crying or anything, if that's what you mean. It's just... well, like I said with the bankers and the photos, I just prefer to stay out of the way. As far as interacting goes, I still talk to people when I absolutely have to, like down at the feed store. \n\nThough, honestly, going down there isn't what it used to be anyway. Old man Miller finally retired last spring and sold the place to some big corporate outfit out of Omaha. Now you go in, and they don't even stock the right sweet feed for the calves anymore. I was trying to explain it to the new manager last week—young kid, maybe twenty-five, wearing these brand-new leather boots that have clearly never seen a day of mud in their life—and I asked him if they could special order the old mix we've used since the nineties. He just pulls out this little tablet screen, taps on it a few times, and tells me it's not in their system. Just like that! Didn't even offer to call the distributor. It's a real shame how all the local places are getting bought up by people who don't know the first thing about livestock. You used to be able to just shake a man's hand and get things done.\n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, interacting with folks. Yeah, I don't know about a lot of emotional distress, I just really hate the thought of people staring at how ruined and cracked my skin is when I'm just trying to do business with them.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid social situations or feel very anxious in settings where people might see your face or hands?\n\nPatient: Well, avoid social situations... I guess you could say that. I mean, I don't go out of my way to be around crowds if I don't have to. Like the church picnic last month. I just couldn't stomach the idea of sitting there with a plate of barbecue while everyone's looking at my hands holding the fork, seeing every little crack and sunspot. \n\nYou know, speaking of barbecue, Pastor Dave always insists on smoking the briskets himself, but he uses this green hickory wood that just gives it this bitter, acrid taste. I tried telling him a few years back that he needs to let the wood season properly—at least six to eight months, really—before he puts it in the smoker. My granddad had a smokehouse out back of the old property, and he'd cure these hams that would just melt in your mouth. He always said the secret was a mix of applewood and just a little bit of cherry. We used to have this huge cherry tree near the front porch... lightning struck it back in '96, split it right down the middle. Broke my heart to chop it up for firewood, but we didn't have much choice. \n\nBut yeah, anxious? I don't know if anxious is the right word. I just don't want people looking at me. So if I don't have to be there, I just stay home.\n\nDoctor: When you're in situations where people might see your face or hands, do you feel very nervous or anxious about how they might react?\n\nPatient: Well, nervous... I guess a little, sure. Mostly I just feel... exposed, you know? Like they're just staring right at the damage. It's why I started wearing those light cotton gloves when I go into town, even in the summer. \n\nThough, finding good cotton gloves anymore is a chore in itself. Used to be you could go down to the hardware store and buy a pack of ten for next to nothing. Good, sturdy canvas ones, too. Now everything's that synthetic nylon stuff that makes your hands sweat out in the fields. I bought a pair last month, and within two days of fixing fence posts, the seams were already blowing out. It's like nobody knows how to stitch a proper seam anymore. My grandmother used to make all our work shirts on this heavy iron Singer machine she had by the window. She could sew through denim like it was butter. I still have one of those shirts somewhere in the attic... probably smells like mothballs and dust now. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, about how people react. Yeah, I don't know if I'm nervous exactly, I just... I just know they're looking, and I hate it. So I just try to keep my head down and get my business done quick.\n\nDoctor: When you're in public and people might see your face or hands, do you feel a strong fear of being judged or embarrassed, or do you mostly just feel uncomfortable with how you look?\n\nPatient: Judged? I don't... I mean, I'm not really worried about them judging me, like they think I'm a bad person or something. It's more just... uncomfortable, like you said. I know how bad the skin looks, all leathery and ruined, and I just don't want them having to look at it. \n\nYou know, speaking of leather, I used to do a bit of tooling myself, back in the day. Made my own belts, even did a saddle once. Takes a lot of patience, getting the moisture just right before you start stamping the pattern. You let it get too dry, and it cracks—just like my hands, I suppose. I had this beautiful set of stamping tools I bought off a fella in Cheyenne... must have been 1982? Yeah, '82, because that was the year the truck broke down on the way back. Blew a head gasket right outside of Laramie. Had to sit at this little diner for six hours waiting for a tow. Best chicken fried steak I ever had, though. The gravy was real thick, not that watery stuff you get out of a packet nowadays. \n\nBut no, I wouldn't say I'm scared of being embarrassed or judged. I just... I know it's ugly, and I just prefer to keep it to myself.\n\nDoctor: When you're in public and can't cover your hands or face, do you feel a strong urge to leave or a sense of panic about how people might react?\n\nPatient: Panic? No, I wouldn't call it panic. I mean, if I'm at the feed store and I realize I left my gloves in the truck, I definitely want to just grab my bags and get out of there as fast as I can. It's just... a really strong urge to just not be seen, you know? \n\nLike last Tuesday, I was picking up some sweet feed for the horses. The guy loading the bags—young kid, maybe nineteen, wearing one of those baseball caps with the flat bill—he kept looking down at my hands when I handed him the receipt. Swore it kept the dust out of his throat. I tried it once, nearly made me sick to my stomach. Tasted like... well, tasted like burnt rubber and molasses. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, leaving. Yeah, if I can't cover up, I just want to finish up and go home. I don't start hyperventilating or anything, I just... I just don't want to linger.\n\nDoctor: When you're in public and people might see your face or hands, do you ever feel a strong fear of being watched, judged, or evaluated by others because of how you look?\n\nPatient: Watched or evaluated? No, I don't think so. I mean, I know they're looking at the damage, but I don't feel like they're evaluating me, like giving me a grade or something. It's not like when the county inspector comes out to look at the new barn. \n\nBoy, that was a headache and a half last spring. The inspector they sent out, this guy named Gary, he had to measure every single joist. Took him three hours. And he had this clipboard that he kept tapping his pen against, click-clack, click-clack. Drove my dog Buster absolutely crazy. Buster's a blue heeler mix, smart as a whip, but he hates repetitive noises. He'll bark at the washing machine if it gets off balance. We had a Maytag for twenty years that finally gave up the ghost, and the new one we got has all these digital buttons that beep every time you touch them. I don't know why a washing machine needs to sound like a video game. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't feel like people are judging me like that. I just... I know my skin looks terrible, and I just prefer to keep it hidden so nobody has to stare at it.\n\nDoctor: When you're in public and people might see your face or hands, do you ever feel a strong sense of anxiety or fear about how you think they might react to your appearance?\n\nPatient: Anxiety or fear about their reaction? I don't know if I'd say anxiety. I mean, I know what they're gonna think because I know what it looks like, right? It's not a mystery. But I wouldn't say I'm sitting there shaking in my boots about it. \n\nYou know, speaking of shaking, we had this old tractor when I was first starting out—an old John Deere, green as a spring apple. The seat on that thing would rattle so hard when you put it in third gear, you'd feel it in your teeth for an hour after you shut it off. My buddy Tom, he tried to fix it by wedging a piece of tire rubber under the mounting bracket. Tom was always doing stuff like that, rigging things up with baling wire and duct tape. He actually built a whole chicken coop using nothing but scrap wood and pallets he found behind the hardware store. Painted the whole thing barn red, but he ran out of paint halfway through the back wall, so it was just bare wood on one side. The chickens didn't seem to mind, though. They laid the biggest brown eggs you ever saw. \n\nBut yeah, back to what you asked... I just don't like them seeing it. It's not a panic attack or anything, I just... I know it's ruined, and I'd rather just keep it covered up.\n\nDoctor: When you're in public and people might see your face or hands, do you ever feel a strong sense of anxiety or fear about how you think they might react to your appearance?\n\nPatient: Didn't we just cover this one, Doc? Like I was saying just a second ago, it's not really fear or anxiety. I just know it looks terrible, so I keep it hidden. It's just practical, you know? Like... well, like throwing a tarp over the hay before a storm. You're not sitting in the house terrified of the rain, you just know what a mess it's gonna make if you don't cover up. \n\nSpeaking of storms, we had a real nasty one roll through last July. Sky turned this weird bruised purple color right around supper time. My wife was making her meatloaf—she puts a little bit of brown sugar in the ketchup glaze, sounds crazy but it's fantastic—anyway, the wind picked up so fast it took the aluminum roof right off the old pump house. Just peeled it back like a sardine can. We found pieces of it three pastures over the next morning. And the hail! Looked like golf balls bouncing off the porch. Dented the hood of my Chevy so bad it looked like someone took a ball-peen hammer to the whole front end. The insurance adjuster they sent out, this fella with a terrible comb-over, tried to tell me it was pre-existing wear and tear. On a truck I bought brand new! I had to get the dealership on the phone just to sort the guy out. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, no anxiety or fear like you're talking about. I just... I guess you could say that. I mean, I don't go out of my way to be around crowds if I don't have to. Like the church picnic last month. I tried telling him a few years back that he needs to let the wood season properly—at least six to eight months, really—before he puts it in the smoker. He always said the secret was a mix of applewood and just a little bit of cherry. We used to have this huge cherry tree near the front porch... lightning struck it back in '96, split it right down the middle. The seat on that thing would rattle so hard when you put it in third gear, you'd feel it in your teeth for an hour after you shut it off. My buddy Tom, he tried to fix it by wedging a piece of tire rubber under the mounting bracket. Tom was always doing stuff like that, rigging things up with baling wire and duct tape. He actually built a whole chicken coop using nothing but scrap wood and pallets he found behind the hardware store. Painted the whole thing barn red, but he ran out of paint halfway through the back wall, so it was just bare wood on one side. The chickens didn't seem to mind, though. They laid the biggest brown eggs you ever saw. \n\nBut yeah, back to what you asked... I just don't like them seeing it. It's not a panic attack or anything, I just... I know it's ruined, and I'd rather just keep it covered up.\n\nDoctor: When you're in public and people might see your face or hands, do you ever feel a strong sense of anxiety or fear about how you think they might react to your appearance?\n\nPatient: Didn't we just cover this one, Doc? Like I was saying just a second ago, it's not really fear or anxiety. I just know it looks terrible, so I keep it hidden. It's just practical, you know? Like... well, like throwing a tarp over the hay before a storm. You're not sitting in the house terrified of the rain, you just know what a mess it's gonna make if you don't cover up. \n\nSpeaking of storms, we had a real nasty one roll through last July. Sky turned this weird bruised purple color right around supper time. My wife was making her meatloaf—she puts a little bit of brown sugar in the ketchup glaze, sounds crazy but it's fantastic—anyway, the wind picked up so fast it took the aluminum roof right off the old pump house. Just peeled it back like a sardine can. We found pieces of it three pastures over the next morning. And the hail! Looked like golf balls bouncing off the porch. Dented the hood of my Chevy so bad it looked like someone took a ball-peen hammer to the whole front end. The insurance adjuster they sent out, this fella with a terrible comb-over, tried to tell me it was pre-existing wear and tear. On a truck I bought brand new! I had to get the dealership on the phone just to sort the guy out. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, no anxiety or fear like you're talking about. I just... I know the damage is there, and I just prefer to keep my hands in my pockets or wear my gloves so nobody has to look at it.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're in public and people might see your face or hands, do you ever feel a strong sense of anxiety or fear about how you think they might react to your appearance?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 62 / miscellaneous_agricultural_worker / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, are your notes mixed up over there? I swear you just asked me that exact same question word for word. You're starting to sound a little like a broken record. Or, well, I guess more like that center pivot irrigation system we had running out in the south field last August. 

The gearbox went out on one of the middle towers, and the whole thing just got stuck in a rut. Kept trying to move but just rocked back and forth, watering the exact same patch of corn for two days straight before I caught it. Turned that whole corner into an absolute mud pit. I had to call my neighbor, Earl, to bring his big Case IH tractor over just to pull the thing out, and even he almost got bogged down in the muck. Earl's a good guy, though. He grows those giant pumpkins every fall for the county fair. Won a blue ribbon three years running until the Peterson brothers brought in one that weighed over a thousand pounds. Lord, they had to haul that monster in on a heavy-duty flatbed trailer. Looked like a bright orange Volkswagen sitting there in the exhibition hall. 

But look, my answer hasn't changed since the last time you asked. I don't get panicky or scared about it. I just know my skin is ruined, and I don't want people staring at the damage, so I keep it covered. That's really all there is to it. Are we sure we're on the right page here?
